My work pants do hang pretty loose now, and people on job sites have said I look too thin, but I kind of figured I’ve just leaned out from being on my feet all day. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, obsessed with it… but yeah, I do worry. If I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger or slower the next day. And with my work, being on ladders and moving around rooms all day, I just… I don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds like, where I can’t stop myself. If anything I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe there are times I eat more than I planned and feel bad about it after, but I wouldn’t call it being out of control. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. To me it feels more like I’m just disciplined, and I’m busy with work, and maybe that makes me smaller than people expect. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I worry I won’t be able to move around at work the way I need to, like on ladders or in rooms.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme… I’m not trying to be difficult. That’s just kind of where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner helps you perform better at your job?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me does think that, yeah. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. But yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is tied to not wanting to gain weight.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. But… yes, other people have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I’m not using laxatives or anything like that after I eat.\n\nI know that’s something people ask about, so… yeah, no. That’s not really part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thin is important for your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I want to look put-together, I guess. I don’t think of it as “being thin is my whole identity” or anything like that.\n\nBut I’d be lying if I said I don’t feel better about myself when I feel smaller or like my clothes fit a certain way. That sounds bad, doesn’t it? I don’t want it to come across like I’m vain. It’s just… it does affect how comfortable I feel being seen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I’m here because the doctor at the clinic was kind of concerned about my eating and thought I should talk to someone. I mean, I don’t think it’s as bad as it probably sounds… most days I just keep things pretty simple. Like coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner if I’m not too busy with class paperwork. That’s… okay to say, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you just not feel like eating most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s kind of a choice, but it doesn’t feel like a big dramatic thing. I just keep it simple most days—coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner later if I’m not tied up with class paperwork. It’s not like I’m trying to make it sound worse than it is… that’s just sort of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know if “enjoy” is the word I’d use. Sometimes if it’s something simple and I’m not feeling rushed, it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I hate it or anything. I just don’t really think about food that way most of the time, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? I haven’t been trying to make a big deal of it. My work pants do hang pretty loose now, and people on job sites have said I look too thin, but I kind of figured I’ve just leaned out from being on my feet all day. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, obsessed with it… but yeah, I do worry. If I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger or slower the next day. And with my work, being on ladders and moving around rooms all day, I just… I don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds like, where I can’t stop myself. If anything I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe there are times I eat more than I planned and feel bad about it after, but I wouldn’t call it being out of control. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. To me it feels more like I’m just disciplined, and I’m busy with work, and maybe that makes me smaller than people expect. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I worry I won’t be able to move around at work the way I need to, like on ladders or in rooms.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme… I’m not trying to be difficult. That’s just kind of where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner helps you perform better at your job?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me does think that, yeah. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. But yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is tied to not wanting to gain weight.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. But… yes, other people have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I’m not using laxatives or anything like that after I eat.\n\nI know that’s something people ask about, so… yeah, no. That’s not really part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thin is important for your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I want to look put-together, I guess. I don’t think of it as “being thin is my whole identity” or anything like that.\n\nBut I’d be lying if I said I don’t feel better about myself when I feel smaller or like my clothes fit a certain way. That sounds bad, doesn’t it? I don’t want it to come across like I’m vain. It’s just… it does affect how comfortable I feel being seen.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything or anything… I’m still going to work, showing up for jobs, and I’m making it to class.\n\nBut, um… I guess it does affect some things. I’ve started skipping lunches with coworkers, or saying no to study meetups, because it feels easier to just keep my routine tight and not have people watching what I’m eating or commenting on it. I know that probably sounds like I’m making it a bigger deal than it is, but… yeah, it has changed what I do socially a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what mainly drives you to limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think that’s part of it, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but if I eat something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nIt’s like this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow, and then I won’t be able to move around the ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. I know that might not sound totally logical, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner if I’m not too busy with class paperwork. That’s… okay to say, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you just not feel like eating most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s kind of a choice, but it doesn’t feel like a big dramatic thing. I just keep it simple most days—coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner later if I’m not tied up with class paperwork. It’s not like I’m trying to make it sound worse than it is… that’s just sort of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know if “enjoy” is the word I’d use. Sometimes if it’s something simple and I’m not feeling rushed, it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I hate it or anything. I just don’t really think about food that way most of the time, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? I haven’t been trying to make a big deal of it. My work pants do hang pretty loose now, and people on job sites have said I look too thin, but I kind of figured I’ve just leaned out from being on my feet all day. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, obsessed with it… but yeah, I do worry. If I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger or slower the next day. And with my work, being on ladders and moving around rooms all day, I just… I don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds like, where I can’t stop myself. If anything I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe there are times I eat more than I planned and feel bad about it after, but I wouldn’t call it being out of control. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. To me it feels more like I’m just disciplined, and I’m busy with work, and maybe that makes me smaller than people expect. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I worry I won’t be able to move around at work the way I need to, like on ladders or in rooms.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme… I’m not trying to be difficult. That’s just kind of where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner helps you perform better at your job?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me does think that, yeah. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I’m not sure how to separate those, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking about the number all the time, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more like… the fear part takes over when I eat something that feels too heavy. Like if I had pizza or a big sandwich, I’d start feeling kind of panicky that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work.

So… I guess it’s less about wanting the weight loss itself, and more about being scared of what gaining would mean. Does that make sense?

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I’m here because the doctor at the clinic was kind of concerned about my eating and thought I should talk to someone. I mean, I don’t think it’s as bad as it probably sounds… most days I just keep things pretty simple. Like coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner if I’m not too busy with class paperwork. That’s… okay to say, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you just not feel like eating most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s kind of a choice, but it doesn’t feel like a big dramatic thing. I just keep it simple most days—coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner later if I’m not tied up with class paperwork. It’s not like I’m trying to make it sound worse than it is… that’s just sort of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know if “enjoy” is the word I’d use. Sometimes if it’s something simple and I’m not feeling rushed, it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I hate it or anything. I just don’t really think about food that way most of the time, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? I haven’t been trying to make a big deal of it. My work pants do hang pretty loose now, and people on job sites have said I look too thin, but I kind of figured I’ve just leaned out from being on my feet all day. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, obsessed with it… but yeah, I do worry. If I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger or slower the next day. And with my work, being on ladders and moving around rooms all day, I just… I don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds like, where I can’t stop myself. If anything I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe there are times I eat more than I planned and feel bad about it after, but I wouldn’t call it being out of control. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. To me it feels more like I’m just disciplined, and I’m busy with work, and maybe that makes me smaller than people expect. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I worry I won’t be able to move around at work the way I need to, like on ladders or in rooms.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme… I’m not trying to be difficult. That’s just kind of where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner helps you perform better at your job?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me does think that, yeah. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. But yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is tied to not wanting to gain weight.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. But… yes, other people have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I’m not using laxatives or anything like that after I eat.\n\nI know that’s something people ask about, so… yeah, no. That’s not really part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thin is important for your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I want to look put-together, I guess. I don’t think of it as “being thin is my whole identity” or anything like that.\n\nBut I’d be lying if I said I don’t feel better about myself when I feel smaller or like my clothes fit a certain way. That sounds bad, doesn’t it? I don’t want it to come across like I’m vain. It’s just… it does affect how comfortable I feel being seen.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything or anything… I’m still going to work, showing up for jobs, and I’m making it to class.\n\nBut, um… I guess it does affect some things. I’ve started skipping lunches with coworkers, or saying no to study meetups, because it feels easier to just keep my routine tight and not have people watching what I’m eating or commenting on it. I know that probably sounds like I’m making it a bigger deal than it is, but… yeah, it has changed what I do socially a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what mainly drives you to limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think that’s part of it, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but if I eat something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nIt’s like this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow, and then I won’t be able to move around the ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. I know that might not sound totally logical, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. It just feels easier to keep my routine kind of controlled and not have people noticing what I’m eating or making comments. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it makes those things less enjoyable, so I avoid them more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is more important to you than the actual weight loss itself?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure how to separate those, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking about the number all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… the fear part takes over when I eat something that feels too heavy. Like if I had pizza or a big sandwich, I’d start feeling kind of panicky that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work.\n\nSo… I guess it’s less about wanting the weight loss itself, and more about being scared of what gaining would mean. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ve started skipping lunches with coworkers, or saying no to study meetups, because it feels easier to just keep my routine tight and not have people watching what I’m eating or commenting on it. I know that probably sounds like I’m making it a bigger deal than it is, but… yeah, it has changed what I do socially a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what mainly drives you to limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think that’s part of it, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but if I eat something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nIt’s like this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow, and then I won’t be able to move around the ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. I know that might not sound totally logical, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped living my life or anything, because I’m still getting to jobs and class.\n\nBut outside of that… yeah, I’ve been saying no to lunches with coworkers and sometimes study meetups. Like if I had pizza or a big sandwich, I’d start feeling kind of panicky that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work.\n\nSo… I guess it’s less about wanting the weight loss itself, and more about being scared of what gaining would mean. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I’m here because the doctor at the clinic was kind of concerned about my eating and thought I should talk to someone. I mean, I don’t think it’s as bad as it probably sounds… most days I just keep things pretty simple. Like coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner if I’m not too busy with class paperwork. That’s… okay to say, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you choose to eat this way or do you just not feel like eating most of the time?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s kind of a choice, but it doesn’t feel like a big dramatic thing. I just keep it simple most days—coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something if I’m between jobs, and then a small dinner later if I’m not tied up with class paperwork. It’s not like I’m trying to make it sound worse than it is… that’s just sort of my routine.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do eat?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know if “enjoy” is the word I’d use. Sometimes if it’s something simple and I’m not feeling rushed, it’s fine. I wouldn’t say I hate it or anything. I just don’t really think about food that way most of the time, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? I haven’t been trying to make a big deal of it. My work pants do hang pretty loose now, and people on job sites have said I look too thin, but I kind of figured I’ve just leaned out from being on my feet all day. Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m, like, obsessed with it… but yeah, I do worry. If I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger or slower the next day. And with my work, being on ladders and moving around rooms all day, I just… I don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds like, where I can’t stop myself. If anything I’m usually pretty aware of what I’m eating. Maybe there are times I eat more than I planned and feel bad about it after, but I wouldn’t call it being out of control. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might eat a normal meal faster than I should if I’m hungry after work, but I wouldn’t say it’s a large amount or anything. I’m usually pretty careful… maybe too careful, I guess. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is a problem?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know other people think it is. I don’t really see my weight as some emergency the way everyone keeps acting like it is. To me it feels more like I’m just disciplined, and I’m busy with work, and maybe that makes me smaller than people expect. But I don’t know… I’m trying to hear what you’re saying, if you think it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the possibility of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to obsess over it, but it does come up. Like if I eat something heavier, like pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling afterward that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I worry I won’t be able to move around at work the way I need to, like on ladders or in rooms.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little extreme… I’m not trying to be difficult. That’s just kind of where my mind goes sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thinner helps you perform better at your job?\n\nPatient: I guess… part of me does think that, yeah. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. But yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is tied to not wanting to gain weight.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. But… yes, other people have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I’m not using laxatives or anything like that after I eat.\n\nI know that’s something people ask about, so… yeah, no. That’s not really part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thin is important for your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I want to look put-together, I guess. I don’t think of it as “being thin is my whole identity” or anything like that.\n\nBut I’d be lying if I said I don’t feel better about myself when I feel smaller or like my clothes fit a certain way. That sounds bad, doesn’t it? I don’t want it to come across like I’m vain. It’s just… it does affect how comfortable I feel being seen.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything or anything… I’m still going to work, showing up for jobs, and I’m making it to class.\n\nBut, um… I guess it does affect some things. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t mean I have to be tiny or anything, but with my job I’m climbing ladders and moving around rooms all day, and I worry if I’m heavier I’ll feel slower or less able to do it.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and just… not move the same. I know that might not be totally logical, but that’s what goes through my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are mostly driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Mostly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like that’s the only thing, because I’m also just trying to function at work. But yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is tied to not wanting to gain weight.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, pizza or a big sandwich, I can get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to. I know that may not make perfect sense, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any significant weight loss that your doctor or others have noticed?\n\nPatient: I mean… people have said things, yeah. Like my work pants are definitely hanging looser than they used to, and a few people on job sites have made comments that I look too thin.\n\nI don’t know if I’d call it significant, exactly. I kind of see it more like I’ve leaned out from being on my feet all day and moving around for work. But… yes, other people have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after eating?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I’m not using laxatives or anything like that after I eat.\n\nI know that’s something people ask about, so… yeah, no. That’s not really part of what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think being thin is important for your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I want to look put-together, I guess. I don’t think of it as “being thin is my whole identity” or anything like that.\n\nBut I’d be lying if I said I don’t feel better about myself when I feel smaller or like my clothes fit a certain way. That sounds bad, doesn’t it? I don’t want it to come across like I’m vain. It’s just… it does affect how comfortable I feel being seen.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight interfere with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s ruining everything or anything… I’m still going to work, showing up for jobs, and I’m making it to class.\n\nBut, um… I guess it does affect some things. I’ve started skipping lunches with coworkers, or saying no to study meetups, because it feels easier to just keep my routine tight and not have people watching what I’m eating or commenting on it. I know that probably sounds like I’m making it a bigger deal than it is, but… yeah, it has changed what I do socially a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is what mainly drives you to limit your food intake?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think that’s part of it, yeah. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but if I eat something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I start worrying almost right away.\n\nIt’s like this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow, and then I won’t be able to move around the ladders and rooms the way I need to for work. I know that might not sound totally logical, but in the moment it feels really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your ability to enjoy or participate in activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped living my life or anything, because I’m still getting to jobs and class.\n\nBut outside of that… yeah, I’ve been saying no to lunches with coworkers and sometimes study meetups. It just feels easier to keep my routine kind of controlled and not have people noticing what I’m eating or making comments. I know that probably sounds kind of silly, but it makes those things less enjoyable, so I avoid them more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is more important to you than the actual weight loss itself?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure how to separate those, exactly. I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking about the number all the time, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more like… the fear part takes over when I eat something that feels too heavy. Like if I had pizza or a big sandwich, I’d start feeling kind of panicky that I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to for work.\n\nSo… I guess it’s less about wanting the weight loss itself, and more about being scared of what gaining would mean. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake even if you knew you wouldn't gain weight?\n\nPatient: I… I think maybe less, if I really knew for sure I wouldn’t gain weight. But I don’t know if I’d completely stop limiting, because the “heavy” feeling still gets to me.\n\nLike with pizza or a big sandwich, even before anything could actually change, I get this panicky thought that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms at work the way I need to. I know that probably sounds a little irrational… but that’s where my mind goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
